ParlaMint-DK_20201203104510	The sitting is resumed. The debate is open. Mrs. Julie Skovsby, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20201203104529	Thank you for the floor. Today we are discussing the draft law amending the law on tests in the church, more specifically the possibility of a phasing-out period. Since the Law on Trials in the People's Church came into force, 11 experimental frameworks have been offered, and the congregation councils have been able to test new frameworks for organizing their tasks to facilitate the work of the councils and to provide a better opportunity for the councils to organise themselves individually. In the first quarter of 2022, the Folkekirkens Uddannelses- og videnscenter makes an overall assessment of all trials. This is done with a view to a possible revision of applicable legislation during the parliamentary year 2022-23. By supplementing with a phasing-out period of 2 years, the result will be that all trial participants will have the opportunity to organise themselves as they have been able during the experiment until the end of the year 2023, i.e. until any new legislation enters into force. It makes sense. From the observations made in the draft law, I would particularly like to highlight regional implications, including for rural areas, where it can be seen that the draft law can have positive rural consequences, insofar as it offers concrete congregational councils, including rural congregation councils, freer in terms of creating an attractive framework for citizens' participation in the church council, which can facilitate sometimes difficult recruitment. This is particularly important in rural areas where the church can be one of few meeting places. For the Social Democratic Party, it is an independent objective to strengthen and strike a balance between the city and the country. There is also full support for the draft law, as all those who have submitted their response to the consultation support the draft. And you can even read about good experience from participation in the experiment and about great satisfaction with the experimental system in the responses received. In those words, I look forward to a good committee reading.

ParlaMint-DK_20201203110220	The debate is open. Secretary of Defense.
ParlaMint-DK_20201203110236	Thank you. The proposal we are discussing here today is about wearing religious symbols in defence, preparedness and police. I have agreed with the Minister of Justice that here today I am also answering on behalf of the police. In the defense, preparedness and police, about 37,000 employees work every day to ensure the Danes' safety. Military employees, police officers and politicians are subject to the uniform rules of the defence, preparedness and police. Uniform is an important part of being a soldier or a police officer. It is part of the professional appearance of the employee and a symbol of the employee's special affiliation. The purpose of the uniform is to create uniformity and recognizability. In addition, the uniform intends to protect the soldier or police officer in the best possible way under all circumstances. Therefore, it is not allowed to mix civilian garments with the uniform. Private clothing does not necessarily have the characteristics compatible with the service. This also means that it is not allowed to wear civilian garments together with the uniform, including garments that are considered religious symbols. The government doesn't want to change the uniform rules in the defense, the preparedness or the police. There are many employees in the defense, the preparedness and the police, and there are probably many desires for how to dress. But uniform rules apply to everyone. It is about security, both for the individual and for the unit. It is important for me to stress that defence, preparedness and the police want to embrace widely and be diverse jobs. Therefore, everyone is encouraged to seek employment regardless of gender, sexuality, ethnicity or religious affiliation. Defence, preparedness and police are high regard for equality and equal treatment. It is about the fact that the strong communities in these institutions are based on values of equality and equal treatment. That is why, for example, common accommodation for men and women has been introduced in military training in defence, which is why the uniform rules apply to everyone. A large proportion of the defense, preparedness and police staff are civilian employees. This means that they are therefore also welcome to wear private garments, which can be regarded as religious symbols, for example. scarf, turban or kalot. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.
ParlaMint-DK_20201203110520	Thank you. There is a brief comment from Mrs Eva Flyvholm.
ParlaMint-DK_20201203110525	I would like to ask the Minister something. Now it is the case that the Swedes, the Norwegians, the Americans and the British allow you to have, for example. scarf together with the uniform; they made it so it fits with the uniform. It is also people we are working with in military operations, so I would like to hear: has it ever given rise to security problems that our allies have this opportunity, for example. To wear a scarf with the uniform?
ParlaMint-DK_20201203110551	The minister.

ParlaMint-DK_20201203111108	Thank you, sir. President. When we send Danish soldiers to war, it is our most important task to ensure their safety. We do this through good, solid training and management and with equipment that supports their tasks. A part of the Danish soldiers' material is among other things. their uniforms. As far as the army is concerned, for example. talk about a uniform with camouflage pattern. The pattern on the uniform is not something we made up for fun. It's a defense against recognition and an important part of the tactics of cloaking the enemy. In addition, it is an important part of the defence, including guidelines for the use of defence equipment, that all soldiers wear the same uniform. There is also an important reason for this. Soldiers must wear the same uniform because it reduces the chance of recognition and thus also reduces the security risk that individual soldiers run when they go to war. That is why the motion for a resolution before us is a shot past the wheel if we are to keep ourselves in a military language. The many civil servants in the defense, preparedness and police are free to wear visible religious garments at work. They can, because they are civilian employees and therefore are not exposed to the same security risks as the Danish soldiers are. With this proposal, the proposers are trying to create an image of a defence where there is no room for everyone. This is a reverse argument. In addition, the proposers are also trying to create an image of a defence in which religion and ethnicity matter to the attachment to the defence. That is not the case, on the contrary. It is precisely because everyone in the defense does not bear visible religious symbols, and it is precisely because everyone in the defense must wear the same uniform that everyone is treated equally. There is room for everyone as well. So it is first and foremost because it is expected that it will pose a security risk to a soldier if, as it were, one sticks out from the crowd and thus also poses a safety risk to the rest of the division in question. Furthermore, the defence actively works to involve, attract and create space for all Danes regardless of ethnicity, religion and sexuality. This proposal will create an increased security risk for our soldiers' lives because of the possibility of recognizability in the field and a reduced possibility of cloaking. In addition, it will also create a defence with a greater focus on the soldiers' background rather than their function in defence. This is a development that we in the Socialist Group do not want. Social democracy wants a defence where all people are welcome and where, regardless of the background, you feel welcome. It works every day for out in the country's regiments and units. On this basis, and in the interests of the safety of soldiers and their ability to act as a unit, the Social Democrats cannot support this proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201203114919	Thank you. This is a proposal that is very unspecific. With this proposal, the government is asked to ensure the necessary initiatives to ensure that you have the opportunity to bear religious symbols if you are part of the defence or police or the preparedness. Free Greens, who have put it forward, state that employees must have the freedom to carry religious symbols, so broadly worded. And there are actually a lot of good considerations in this proposal when you read the comments. In the SF, we agree with many of them, among others. that Danish society is diverse and multicultural, and that we should embrace it. We also agree that there are limitations for minorities who bear religious symbols when it comes to participation in the defence, and we ourselves have problematized it in the past. However, as the proposal stands here, I cannot really support it, and there are several reasons for this. First of all, I note that the debate places a great deal of emphasis on, among other things, the desire of the Member States. the measures that are in the United States, in the British defence, in Norway, and that they say that if they can, then we can. And that is true, but if we take the example of the United States, it is not the case that you have the freedom, as an employee of the defence, for example, to wear religious symbols. It's something you have to apply for. When you have done this in the United States at some point, it is because you have positive experiences with several different religious symbols, but not all of them. And there are limitations on what types you allow. For example, the Americans have had major problems with religious minorities who would like to have beard growth as part of their religious practice. This has created some limitations for e.g.: safety masks and other equipment in which it actually poses a safety risk in relation to its use. That is why, when these measures were introduced in the United States, some money was set aside to test both existing equipment and alternative equipment in order to make it work and identify areas where there is a safety risk, among other things. for beard-growers because it exists in such a way; there are places where it is a security risk. That is not included in this proposal, and I hear the parties in particular. The line of defense doesn't sound like it's something you're going to set aside money for. And I actually see that as a little problematic. I also think we should recognise that not all religious symbols will be able to go in relation to this, but it is true that some people will, and we must somehow have identified that before we can just oblige the government to do so here. So I have sympathy for the proposal, but I think it is too unspecific, and it lacks the perspective that when other countries have this opportunity, it is not something that is as broad and general as it is formulated here. This is something that in many cases requires applications and where there are a lot of reservations and exceptions. And I really think we should take them seriously. I do not think I really hear that in the debate, either on the one hand or on the other, because it will be very valuable without really dealing with what is technically possible. I think that is a pity, because I might actually have thought of a good qualified debate on this, perhaps under the authority of the Ministry. However, as the proposal stands here, the SF cannot support it.

The Commission criticises, among other things: In several places, the Danish implementation does not contain a precise representation of the text of the directive. In several places it is pointed out that implementation is incomplete, although the missing information is described in the legislative notes. Finally, the Commission points out in some places that implementation is not correct, particularly with regard to the implementation of the definition of the public and the consultation provisions. ParlaMint-DK_20201203124047	Thank you for the floor. Today we are discussing two motions for resolutions on this subject, B 26, and the subsequent B 27 which, of course, have a certain relationship with each other. Nevertheless, there is also a slight difference in relation to proposers, etc. It is hardly a secret what the government's position is on this issue, since it has not been so long since we last dealt with a similar motion for a resolution, namely in the spring, in the last session of the Assembly, and therefore there will also be elements of what I am saying today which may be repeated. I hope you understand that. The proposal, B 26, requires the government to put forward a legislative proposal to abolish the area reservations in the northern part of the Ring 5 transport corridor. Transport corridors in the metropolitan area are area reservations that regulate the use of the areas so that future infrastructure facilities can be placed in as little conflict as possible with urban development, thus ensuring that social costs and interventions in urban environment and landscape are minimised. I would like to emphasise that no decision has been taken as to whether a motorway should be set up in the Ring 5 corridor. The motion for a resolution here is therefore aimed at abolishing land reservations in the northern part of the transport corridor and eliminating any motorway plans, while preserving part of the corridor within a few metres for future energy infrastructure projects. The reason for the proposal is that the reservation has been in place for nearly 50 years and imposes severe restrictions on citizens and authorities in the municipalities concerned. In addition, the explanatory statement also mentions that the northern part will have environmental damage to nature areas on the stretch, including for Great Dyrehave, Mølleådalen and the nature park between Farum and Slangerup. Furthermore, the proposals are justified by the fact that a motorway will lead to a traffic jump, which will increase Denmark's emissions and thus make it more difficult to achieve the goal of reducing emissions of CO2 When we look at the traffic challenges in the metropolitan area, all the indications are that in the coming years we will face challenges with congestion, especially on the ring connections, and the problem will not be reduced if we increase the capacity of what is called fingers in the finger plan, that is to say the fingers that feed on ring connections. Unfortunately, collective traffic cannot solve all traffic needs, and the ring links in the metropolitan area have a relatively poor public transport service. This is also something that the Commission of Tribulation has pointed out in the past, and besides the light rail in Ring 3, there are no concrete plans for the expansion of high-class public transport in the rings. That is why there is a fundamental need to look at the development of roads in ring links. However, this does not mean that the establishment of a motorway in Ring 5 is the only option. This is also the case, the last time we dealt with a similar proposal, a two-part analysis of the north and the south of the transport corridor respectively. I shall return to the examination of the southern part of the corridor in connection with the next resolution. The feasibility study in the northern part of the corridor has been initiated to improve the decision-making basis for the possible further existence of the transport corridor and to provide the basis for the decision on a possible narrowing of the corridor. In the preliminary study of the northern corridor, it is assumed that the corridor will proceed at the end of a southern Ring 5 on the Frederikssund motorway in the south and towards a connection to the Helsingør motorway in the north. In order to assess the potential for narrowing the corridor, analyses shall be made of the possibilities for the location of the motorway or other facilities in order to reduce the areas in the corridor where it is not considered appropriate to locate a motorway. More concrete analyses will also be carried out in relation to properties in the corridor. The analyses were launched in the spring of 2019 as a result of the green conciliation with the agreement of 26 August 2016 on the mobilisation of funds for road projects at Haderup and Ribe, etc., where it was agreed to discuss the organisation of a planning study on the Ring 5 Corridor. There have always been many opinions about whether we should maintain the current transport corridor in Ring 5 or not, and there are just as many opinions about whether there should be a motorway in the corridor or whether the traffic challenges in the capital should be solved in other ways. I would like to stress once again that neither the final position of the government nor the views of other parties on a motorway link in Ring 5 depend on whether or not the current analysis of the corridor is supported. We in the government believe it is important that the decision on area reservations, whether in the north or in the south, is taken on a solid and enlightened basis. That is why the resolution must include the analyses carried out by the Road Directorate and, on that basis, the government cannot support this motion for a resolution.

For SF there is one final argument, which is that building motorways towards Copenhagen to solve a congestion problem is that there is an increasing proportion of cars that would like to enter Copenhagen, especially during peak times, and it is therefore felt that another motorway should be built to solve the problem we see is unsustainable, especially during a period in which we have to resolve a climate crisis, and especially bearing in mind that we can see that we do not solve congestion problems by building more motorways. In fact, the opposite usually happens, i.e. when we build a motorway towards Copenhagen, it generates more congestion. For example, we saw this with Ring 3, which was badly completed before the tribulation had exceeded what it could actually cope with. That's why in our eyes it's a complete hole in the head to have this transport corridor, even in the case that there's an alternative called Ring 51⁄2 or Call 6. That is why I think this is an excellent proposal which not only takes account of the citizens who have had uncertainty for a very long time, but also takes account of the climate and takes into account the obvious fact that when we have to solve a congestion problem around Copenhagen, the solution is not yet another motorway.

ParlaMint-DK_20201203141700	Thank you, President. We shall now proceed to the debate on motion for a resolution No. B 27 there will probably be a bit of repetitions. Nevertheless, with the motion for a resolution, the proposers are instructing the government to table a legislative proposal abolishing the area reservations in the southern part of the Ring 5 transport corridor. Transport corridors in the metropolitan area are area reservations that regulate the use of the areas so that future infrastructure facilities can be placed in as little conflict as possible with urban development. This ensures that social costs and interventions in the urban environment and landscape are minimised. And I would like to emphasise very clearly that there is no analysis of the corridor where we also look at a number of alternatives to a motorway connection. I will come back to that in a moment. The motion for a resolution here is therefore that land reservations for the transport corridor should be abolished and any motorway plans dropped, while part of the corridor of a few metres' width should be preserved for future energy infrastructure projects. The reason for the proposal is that the reservation has been in place for nearly 50 years and imposes severe restrictions on citizens and authorities in the municipalities concerned. In addition, this proposal is also justified by the fact that a motorway will give a traffic jump that will increase Denmark's emissions of CO2 2 In the metropolitan area, everything suggests that in the coming years we are facing major challenges with congestion, especially in the ring connections. The problem will not be reduced if, at the same time, we increase the capacity of our fingers, that is to say, those who give birth to ring links. Unfortunately, collective traffic cannot solve all traffic needs, and the ring links in the metropolitan area have a relatively poor public transport service. This is also something that the Commission of Tribulation has pointed out in the past. Apart from the light rail in Ring 3, there are no concrete plans for the expansion of high-quality public transport in the rings, and therefore there is a fundamental need to look at the development of roads in ring links. However, this does not mean that the establishment of a motorway in Ring 5 is the only possible solution. A two-part analysis of the northern and southern part of the transport corridor is carried out. The study of the southern corridor has been initiated, among other things. to provide an overview of the location of any future installations. It also looks at the link between modes of transport, including the location of hubs with the possibility of switching between modes of transport, as well as an assessment of whether bus lanes on motorways with stops and access to interconnections can give a traffic effect. In addition, access to and from super bike paths is assessed at co-deck sites. Finally, there is also an analysis of prospects for public transport in the rings, as well as whether the capacity and frequency of S-trains can have a traffic effect in relation to the location of nodes. The analyses were launched in the spring of 2019 as a result of the green conciliation with the agreement of 26 August 2016 on the mobilisation of funds for road projects at Haderup and Ribe etc., and here it was agreed to discuss the organisation of a planning study on the Ring 5 Corridor. The analysis in the southern part is supported financially by a number of surrounding municipalities. There have been many opinions over the years as to whether or not we should maintain the current transport corridor in Ring 5, and there are just as many opinions as to whether there should be a motorway in the corridor or whether the traffic solutions in the capital should be solved in other ways. I would like to stress once again that neither the final position of the government nor the views of other parties on a motorway link in Ring 5 depend on whether or not the current analysis of the corridor is supported. We in the government believe it is important that a decision on area reservations, whether in the north or in the south, is taken on a solid and enlightened basis. Especially now that there are also municipalities which are involved in financing the analysis, we also think it is most reasonable to wait for the completion of these analyses carried out by the Road Directorate. Against this background, the government cannot support motion for a resolution No. B 27.

ParlaMint-DK_20201203143706	Thank you. As I talked about before, Denmark has some fantastic natural areas that we need to secure and preserve. It is therefore a transport corridor with area reservations that has been tied to people for nearly 50 years, and of course New Citizens cannot support it. Sjælland and Nordsjælland should not, of course, act as a bypass for heavy transport between Sweden and Germany. Danish infrastructure is something that must bind the country together. Therefore, we will remove the user fee on the Storebælt Bridge and on Crown Princess Mary's Bridge in Frederikssund. We will improve and expand the road network in all parts of Denmark: Hillerød Motorway, Frederikssund Motorway, between Næstved and Rønnede, extend the E45 all the way to the border, secure a third Limfjord connection and much, much more. We want traffic equality so that it is not more expensive to sail 1 km than it is to drive 1 km, to all non-bridged islands. In short, infrastructure must bind Denmark much better together. I have experienced on my own body the sufferings that children with special needs have suffered from having to be included in a general environment, and how they were not included excluded The fine teaching words and pussies had been wrapped around the law as sugar to make the bitter medicine slide down. But the politicians didn't trick any of us out there in the real world. A law such as the Inclusion Act is only something that can be born behind the thick walls of Christiansborg and the Ministry of Finance, far away from reality, far away from all those affected by the consequences. I have spoken against this harmful law from the outset, and now we are here today with an excellent proposal from the Enhedslisten, namely to ensure that parents who receive less than the 9 o'clock or 12 lessons' support for their child can also complain. We guarantee their legal certainty and, of course, we fully support that. The Complaints Board for Special Education's Annual Report shows that out of the 177 substantive complaints in the field of primary school, the municipality's decision was amended in 34 per cent. of the cases, while the denominator in 10%. of the cases referred the case back to the municipality with notice of re-examination. Action is really needed. The fact is that inclusion is important, but it was already before the harmful law, then we just didn't call it inclusion, because then it was just common sense that the children who could be in the general school were there and received the offer or help they needed, and those who couldn't be there, well they got an offer based on their special needs, so they could be part of an inclusive community at a special school. And that's the way it's supposed to be. The assessment must always be based on the individual child, which means that the majority of children at least of the children I have met, many of those with multiple diagnoses, who can easily form part of a community at a school. And it's not because they don't want to; all children just want to fit in, all parents I've met just want their child to fit in. But it is simply because they cannot. What is happening is not inclusion, when a law such as the Act of 2012 includes on the basis of an excelark and not on the basis of the needs of the individual child. We certainly believe that parents of children who receive less than 9 hours or 12 lessons should be able to complain, and that is why we also support this motion for a resolution and urge the inclusionist politicians from the very heart to take a trip out in reality, out there, where the inclusion law has real, serious human consequences. Thank you.

Here it is the government's clear position that social media should also meet with very clear demands that reflect the importance and gravity of the public debate when using social media. The second working group will look at how we can ensure children and young people's digital lives and education on the Internet and reduce their exposure to harmful content. Among other things, it must: make suggestions on how we can better equip children and young people to act online, and see if we can make our vision sharper on the pitfalls that are now, but also in relation to predicting those that are coming. ParlaMint-DK_20201203182519	The proposal, as my fellow rapporteur said earlier, is that refugees on temporary stays, the so-called § 7 para graphs. 3 refugees and families brought together, will have access to vocational training without self-payment. Perhaps there is reason to start by saying what the whole reason why we have a category of refugees, which is called Section 7, paragraph 7. Three is. It is that the then government under Prime Minister Helle Thorning-Schmidt believed that some of the refugees who came to Denmark were here for such a short time that there was no reason to include them in the ordinary refugee process. So there were convention refugees pursuant to § 7 para. 1 and there were refugees who came for other reasons under § 7 (1). 2 and then there were those who should only be here for a very short time, according to § 7 para. 3. This proposal is to do it free of charge for the latter group, as per per. definition is considered to only be here for a short time, that is to say they must now have a free vocational education in Denmark. I find that contradictory. That seems to the Left to be contradictory. In fact, when we were in government, we tried to stress that granting asylum in accordance with Article 7 (7). 3, precisely targeted refugees, who should be here for a short time. And the government's change this summer that it would now be free for this group to go to higher education was wrong. And this decision today is also wrong. In fact, we on the Left believe that if you are to have a section 7 paragraph 7 paragraph 7. It is also important for us to signal to them that their stay in Denmark should be shorter and that they should prepare to return to the country from which they have fled. For the whole idea behind § 7 para. 3 is that it is for some people who have come here from a conflict, which we assess because it is after all the Danish authorities who consider that they should have a stay according to § 7 paragraph 3. 3 is a conflict that allows them to return quickly. I recognise, of course, that there may be some who come here and get asylum according to § 7 para. 3, after which the conflict does not prove to be short-lived and there can then come a challenge with how to deal with them. But you send the wrong signal by saying in advance that when you come here and are judged to have asylum for a short time in Denmark, you get free access to a vocational training or, for that matter, a long higher education; that is the wrong signal to send. We in the Left would like to maintain that Denmark is a country that treats asylum seekers properly, but we have just chosen to have different categories of asylum because asylum seekers come into different groups. And that is why we are opposed to sending the signal to those who come up here and get asylum on a short-term basis that they can also start vocational training in Denmark. I believe that this asylum proposal will help to make it more attractive to seek asylum in Denmark rather than in other countries. The Danish Parliament notes that integration problems with the forced distribution of Danish children cannot be solved. ParlaMint-DK_20201203200311	Thank you, Mr President. I would like to start by thanking the authors of the proposal for a mapping of persecution of Christians in the countries with which Denmark cooperates. Many international reports document that the extent of discrimination against and persecution of Christians in the world is not only complex and large, but also, unfortunately, increasing. It is therefore natural and right that we here in the Folketing should focus on the problem, its scope and character. I see an independent point in that, because if there is something the government is concerned with, it is precisely these issues too. I believe that we have an obligation to show solidarity with those people in the world who, because of their beliefs and beliefs, must live with discrimination, oppression and persecution, yes, sometimes even mass murder and annihilation. When I took over the position of Foreign Minister, I decided to continue the special effort for freedom of religion and belief and persecuted religious minorities, including specifically persecuted Christians, because the number of Christians discriminated against and persecuted on a global scale is increasing and because discrimination is terrible and must be combated, whether it be based on religion, skin colour, gender, sexual orientation, or whatever one might find out of outrageous excuses to discriminate against one's fellow human beings. That is why we have spread our work to include atheists and other unbelievers in addition to the persecuted Christians. Atheists are particularly persecuted in countries where it is forbidden to criticise religion or simply to think that God does not exist, because this in itself is considered blasphemy ~ a crime that in several Muslim countries can lead to the death penalty. It is often the case that if atheists or religious minorities are discriminated against or persecuted in a country, Christians usually are. This means that when we talk about the rights of atheists or Yazidians, we are of course also talking about the rights of Christians. The good thing about starting from the freedom of thought, conscience and religion is that it's all about us all. In view of the increase we have seen in anti-Semitism globally, in Europe and, unfortunately, at home too, we have also given priority to the government's efforts against anti-Semitism. We do, among other things. in the International Holocaust Remembrance Alliance, the IHRA as we shorten what works for the Holocaust to never go into oblivion. And in January, I myself took part in the selection of the 20th anniversary of the important IHRA cooperation. The special unit for freedom of religion and belief has been active in the work we have had between ministries with the national action plan to combat anti-Semitism. The action plan is expected to be launched next year. Jewish persecution and the Holocaust constitute some of the most absolutely darkest chapters in human history, and that is what happened here in Europe. We have a special obligation to our Jewish fellow citizens and countrymen to ensure that they can live and move freely and safely in Denmark. The words never again mean that we must use our Danish experience to fight for the absence of new genocide or persecution. We must prevent new genocide by involving religious actors as part of the solution. We must promote respect for the right of all minorities to be citizens on an equal footing with everyone else. We must combat religious nationalism, intolerance and fanaticism. This government wants to pursue a clear value-based foreign policy where human rights are central. We are in the midst of a very active Danish membership of the UN Human Rights Council. Here, Denmark went to elections for the fight against torture, the rights of indigenous peoples, the rights of women and girls and freedom of religion and belief. This does not mean that only these human rights are important. All rights are important, interrelated and cannot be ranked, but the right to freedom of religion and belief has been the somewhat neglected right. Many have thought that it would become irrelevant as we became more modern and the importance of religions would diminish. The real world has turned out to be something else. At this time, when we are once again suffering from terrorist attacks by militant Islamists in Europe, it is obvious that freedom of expression is inextricably linked to freedom of religion and belief, because a prerequisite for free expression is also that one can think freely, including choosing one's own religion or opting out entirely from religion. Freedom of religion and belief is not the right of religion, but primarily the right of the individual. Religious and religious freedom cannot be used to shield itself from criticism or even ridicule. The government shares the concern about the many serious violations of religious and religious freedom around the world. This is particularly true of the persecution of Christians, but it is also true of atheists, Jews, Hindus, Muslims and other minorities. When a minority suffers persecution, it also affects others: if we are to help Christians, we must safeguard the rights of all minorities. Everyone must be guaranteed rights as equal citizens. This does not mean that we do not have a special eye on the most persecuted, the Christians, because we do. There are already a large number of international reports which document violations of religious and religious freedom and Christian persecution globally and at country level: Pew Research's reports, State Department's country reports, the reports of the United States Congress' Commission on International Religious Freedom, as well as reports from faith-based organisations such as the United States Congress. Open Doors, Church in Need and Humanists International, whose 2020 report is published here on 10 December. They're all documenting these different conditions. And that was among other things. on the basis of these that the Bishop of Truro prepared his own report on Christian persecution to the British Foreign Ministry. To repeat the exercise proposed by the authors of the motion for a resolution will, in my opinion, hardly contribute significantly new. In other words, we know what the problem is. It is obvious and quite well documented already. So the question is: how do we ensure in cooperation a positive change in the world? I am very keen that Denmark should contribute to the work of focusing on Christian persecutions in the world, and I would like to do so in a way that makes the best possible use of the resources we have. Denmark is already boxing in several areas above its weight class. In three years we have seen a significant international mobilisation about the right to freedom of religion and belief. Three meetings of Foreign Ministers have been held since 2018 with a focus on freedom of religion and belief. The latest event took place only on 16 November, when I myself took part in speeches. In my speech, I took as a starting point the terrorist attacks in France and the right to freedom of expression. As you know, the United States has launched an international alliance to promote freedom of religion and belief. The freedom not to believe is explicitly shown by the name and declaration of principle of the alliance, and that is something that we on the Danish side made conditional on joining this alliance. So this is a concrete Danish print and something that Denmark has received a lot of praise for. Another issue we have highlighted in particular is the suppression of women, girls and other sexual orientations by religions. Freedom of religion and belief and gender equality do not stand in opposition, on the contrary. Together with the Netherlands and the UK, Denmark has been the banner leader in the FoRB alliance to ensure that the positive synergies between FoRB and equality are recognised as important areas of work, and this is therefore not straightforward at this time. In fact, we are already working closely with the UK, in other words with Britain, in the field of freedom of religion and belief. And one of the good things about the alliance is that we complement each other. We must also recognise that the British foreign service is larger than ours and that we, for example, are. do not have the permanent place in the United Nations Security Council, as the British have, and referred to in the report by the Bishop of Truro. The opportunity to make a difference to the faith-based minorities, including the Christians, has obviously for most of the year been marked by the covid-19 restrictions. A number of the dialogues with countries and religious institutions where there are challenges have had to be postponed, but this has not meant that everything has stalled. For example: I raised the issue of religious minorities with my Egyptian Foreign Minister colleague when I was recently in Cairo. Similarly, later this month, the government participates in the Contact Forum to discuss the results of the megaphone survey that the Think Tank for Persecution Christians has been launched and financed, and this is exciting reading. Freedom of speech is the right that the Danes think is most important. Perhaps we already knew that it was something we intuitively thought was such, because we know how treasured that right is here in Denmark. But that freedom of religion, thought and faith is the second most important, few expected. This underpins our priorities. The study also shows that we need to improve public information. For example, the Danes believe that Muslims are the most persecuted in the world, and the Danes believe that the Muslims, next to China, are the most persecuted in the United States followed by Denmark. It's totally wrong when you look at reality. In other words, people need to learn more about the factual conditions in the world, and we are happy to help in the Contact Forum and elsewhere. This was also indicated by the former Foreign Minister at his meeting with the think tank in October. We have a lot of work to do outside, but also at home. We have a good strategy, and its starting point, i.e. in the right of thought, conscience and religion, has given Denmark's efforts credibility and impact. It is important that the effort is not seen as populist or one-sidedly focused on Christian interests, then we lose the power of action that we need in the world to make a real difference to minorities; then our efforts will be less effective. And it is with this background that the government cannot endorse the motion for a resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party. Thank you.

Many Christians today live in contexts where they are in a minority, either as a special Christian congregation in a society in which another Christian church has the majority, or as Christians in a society with a completely different religious culture. Often it is the case that the majority who govern those societies discriminate or persecute Christian minorities. It can be through laws and bans on a religious practice other than official and with prosecution as a consequence of the violation; it can be by allowing discrimination by the police or the religious majority against Christians; it can be the discrimination of the general community that excludes Christian minorities from the labour market and other social integration; and finally it can be violent assaults and murders of Christian minorities. The persecution of Christian religious minorities is a problem for the individual believer. And that is a major problem for the entire Christian minority. In New Citizens we have the attitude not to interfere untimely with the internal religious challenges, customs and cultures of other countries, in the same way as we do not think it appropriate for other countries to interfere in Danish relations. However, in relation to the core of the matter, structural, state and civil persecution of Christian minorities in countries benefiting from cooperation with Denmark, we find that development very problematic. The present motion for a resolution that the Danish Parliament instructs the government to draw up a report identifying the persecution of Christians in the countries with which Denmark cooperates is a step in the right direction. That is why new citizens can support this motion for a resolution.
